# Crafting Interpreters — annotated link catalog

Author: Robert Nystrom. Source: [online table of contents](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/contents.html>).

Captured: 2026-11-08T11:47:08.461917+00:00.

**Coverage:** 39 exported book pages; 1831 hyperlink occurrences; 648 distinct absolute destinations.

**What “all links” means:** every nonempty HTTP, HTTPS, or email hyperlink in the exported HTML pages, including prose, code links, asides, challenges, design notes, headings, menus, and footers. Repeated destinations appear once below, with their source sections listed. Section fragments are kept distinct. Script controls with no destination, CSS/font/image resource URLs that are not hyperlinks, and links on external sites are not recursively collected.

**Descriptions:** internal links are described using their target heading and opening text. External links are described using the anchor text and the surrounding book passage; that contextual passage is included. External pages were not independently read or checked for availability, so this catalog does not claim their current contents have been verified.

**PDF behavior:** links to other webpages remain online; same-page section links are PDF-internal destinations where Chromium supports them. This Markdown file also gives the original web URL for every section.

## External references and email links

138 distinct destinations.

### E004. the Curry-Howard isomorphism

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curry%E2%80%93Howard_correspondence>)

**About:** The correspondence between propositions and types, and between proofs and programs; background for the relationship between type systems and formal reasoning.

**Book context:** In the early half of last century, Haskell Curry and William Alvin Howard showed that they are two sides of the same coin: the Curry-Howard isomorphism .

**Linked from:** [Introduction](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html>)

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compilers:_Principles,_Techniques,_and_Tools>)

**Book context:** Compilers: Principles, Techniques, and Tools (universally known as “the dragon book”) is the canonical reference.

**Linked from:** [Introduction / 1 . 1 . 3 One more reason](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html>); [Scanning / 4 . 3 Regular Languages and Expressions](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/scanning.html>); [Scanning on Demand / 16 . 4 . 1 Tries and state machines](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/scanning-on-demand.html>)

### E007. Lex

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lex_(software)>)

**About:** A lexical-analyzer generator that produces scanners from token-pattern specifications.

**Book context:** Many other language books and language implementations use tools like Lex and Yacc , so-called compiler-compilers , that automatically generate some of the source files for an implementation from some higher-level description.

**Linked from:** [Introduction / 1 . 2 . 1 The code](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html>); [Scanning on Demand / 16 . 4 . 1 Tries and state machines](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/scanning-on-demand.html>)

### E008. Yacc

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yacc>)

**About:** A parser generator that produces parsing code from a grammar; its name means Yet Another Compiler-Compiler.

**Book context:** Many other language books and language implementations use tools like Lex and Yacc , so-called compiler-compilers , that automatically generate some of the source files for an implementation from some higher-level description.

**Linked from:** [Introduction / 1 . 2 . 1 The code](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html>)

**Linked from:** [Introduction / 1 . 2 . 1 The code](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html>)

### E013. GIMPLE

[Open resource](<https://gcc.gnu.org/onlinedocs/gccint/GIMPLE.html>)

**About:** GCC’s GIMPLE intermediate representation, used between language-specific front ends and later compilation stages.

**Book context:** Language front ends target one of a handful of IRs, mainly GIMPLE and RTL .

### E014. RTL

[Open resource](<https://gcc.gnu.org/onlinedocs/gccint/RTL.html>)

**About:** GCC’s Register Transfer Language, a lower-level intermediate representation describing operations closer to target machine instructions.

**Book context:** Language front ends target one of a handful of IRs, mainly GIMPLE and RTL .

**Linked from:** [A Map of the Territory / 2 . 1 . 4 Intermediate representations](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>)

### E015. x86

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/X86>)

**About:** The x86 processor instruction-set family, used as an example of architecture-specific native-code targets.

**Book context:** If your compiler targets x86 machine code, it’s not going to run on an ARM device.

**Linked from:** [A Map of the Territory / 2 . 1 . 6 Code generation](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>)

### E016. ARM

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ARM_architecture>)

**About:** The ARM processor architecture family, contrasted with x86 to explain why native code is architecture-dependent.

**Book context:** If your compiler targets x86 machine code, it’s not going to run on an ARM device.

**Linked from:** [A Map of the Territory / 2 . 1 . 6 Code generation](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>)

### E017. AAD

[Open resource](<http://www.felixcloutier.com/x86/AAD.html>)

**About:** The x86 AAD instruction reference: ASCII Adjust AX Before Division, an example of a specialized legacy instruction.

**Book context:** For example, the AAD (“ASCII Adjust AX Before Division”) instruction lets you perform division, which sounds useful.

**Linked from:** [A Map of the Territory / 2 . 1 . 6 Code generation](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>)

### E018. Go tool

[Open resource](<https://golang.org/>)

**About:** The Go programming language and toolchain; the book contrasts building an executable with compiling and immediately running it.

**Book context:** The Go tool is even more of a horticultural curiosity.

**Linked from:** [A Map of the Territory / 2 . 1 . 8 Runtime](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>); [A Map of the Territory / 2 . 3 Compilers and Interpreters](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>)

### E019. Syntax-directed translation

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syntax-directed_translation>)

**About:** A compilation technique that associates semantic actions, such as code generation, with grammar productions.

**Book context:** Syntax-directed translation is a structured technique for building these all-at-once compilers.

### E020. almost every language out there

[Open resource](<https://github.com/jashkenas/coffeescript/wiki/list-of-languages-that-compile-to-js>)

**About:** A community-maintained list of languages and compilers that target JavaScript.

**Book context:** Web browsers are the “machines” of today, and their “machine code” is JavaScript, so these days it seems almost every language out there has a compiler that targets JS since that’s the main way to get your code running in a browser.

### E021. WebAssembly

[Open resource](<https://github.com/webassembly/>)

**About:** The WebAssembly project, providing a low-level compilation target that can execute in web browsers.

**Book context:** Thanks to WebAssembly , compilers now have a second, lower-level language they can target that runs on the web.

**Linked from:** [A Map of the Territory / 2 . 2 . 3 Transpilers](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>)

### E022. declaration reflects use

[Open resource](<http://softwareengineering.stackexchange.com/questions/117024/why-was-the-c-syntax-for-arrays-pointers-and-functions-designed-this-way>)

**About:** Discussion of C declaration syntax and the “declaration reflects use” design principle for pointers, arrays, and functions.

**Book context:** Dennis Ritchie had this idea called “ declaration reflects use ”, where variable declarations mirror the operations you would have to perform on the variable to get to a value of the base type.

**Linked from:** [The Lox Language / 3 . 1 Hello, Lox](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html>)

### E025. classes

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Class-based_programming>)

### E026. prototypal inheritance

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prototype-based_programming>)

**About:** Object-oriented programming that reuses behavior through prototype objects rather than requiring class definitions.

**Book context:** The Lua implementation refers to the raw function object containing the bytecode as a “prototype”, which is a great word to describe this, except that word also gets overloaded to refer to prototypal inheritance .

**Linked from:** [The Lox Language / 3 . 9 . 3 Classes or prototypes](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html>); [Classes / Design Note: Prototypes and Power](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/classes.html>); [Closures / 25 . 1 Closure Objects](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/closures.html>)

### E027. pushes you pretty hard

[Open resource](<http://gameprogrammingpatterns.com/prototype.html#what-about-javascript>)

**About:** The Prototype chapter of Game Programming Patterns, exploring object cloning and prototype-based reuse.

**Book context:** JavaScript’s “constructor function” notion pushes you pretty hard towards defining class-like objects.

**Linked from:** [The Lox Language / 3 . 9 . 3 Classes or prototypes](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html>)

### E029. waterbed theory

[Open resource](<http://wiki.c2.com/?WaterbedTheory>)

**About:** The “waterbed theory” of complexity: simplifying one part of a language or system can move unavoidable complexity elsewhere.

**Book context:** Larry Wall, Perl’s inventor/prophet calls this the “ waterbed theory ”.

**Linked from:** [The Lox Language / 3 . 9 . 3 Classes or prototypes](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html>)

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regular_language>)

**About:** Formal languages recognizable by finite automata and describable by regular expressions; relevant to token scanning.

### E032. Chomsky hierarchy

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chomsky_hierarchy>)

**About:** The classification of formal grammars and languages by expressive power, from regular to unrestricted grammars.

**Book context:** It pains me to gloss over the theory so much, especially when it’s as interesting as I think the Chomsky hierarchy and finite-state machines are.

**Linked from:** [Scanning / 4 . 3 Regular Languages and Expressions](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/scanning.html>); [Representing Code](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/representing-code.html>)

### E033. finite-state machines

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finite-state_machine>)

**About:** A computational model with a finite collection of states and transitions, relevant to scanners and regular languages.

**Book context:** It pains me to gloss over the theory so much, especially when it’s as interesting as I think the Chomsky hierarchy and finite-state machines are.

**Linked from:** [Scanning / 4 . 3 Regular Languages and Expressions](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/scanning.html>)

[Open resource](<http://dinosaur.compilertools.net/lex/>)

**About:** Documentation for Lex, a tool that generates lexical analyzers from regular-expression token specifications.

**Book context:** Tools like Lex or Flex are designed expressly to let you do this — throw a handful of regexes at them, and they give you a complete scanner back .

[Open resource](<https://github.com/westes/flex>)

**Book context:** Tools like Lex or Flex are designed expressly to let you do this — throw a handful of regexes at them, and they give you a complete scanner back .

**Linked from:** [Scanning / 4 . 3 Regular Languages and Expressions](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/scanning.html>)

[Open resource](<https://www.lua.org/pil/1.1.html>)

### E038. Go

[Open resource](<https://golang.org/ref/spec#Semicolons>)

**About:** Go’s lexical rules for semicolons and automatic insertion at selected line endings.

**Book context:** Go handles newlines in the scanner.

**Linked from:** [Scanning / Design Note: Implicit Semicolons](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/scanning.html>)

### E039. gofmt

[Open resource](<https://golang.org/cmd/gofmt/>)

**About:** gofmt, Go’s canonical source formatter, mentioned alongside Go’s newline and semicolon rules.

**Book context:** The Go team provides a canonical code formatter, gofmt , and the ecosystem is fervent about its use, which ensures that idiomatic styled code works well with this simple rule.

**Linked from:** [Scanning / Design Note: Implicit Semicolons](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/scanning.html>)

### E040. Python

[Open resource](<https://docs.python.org/3.5/reference/lexical_analysis.html#implicit-line-joining>)

**About:** Python’s implicit line-joining rules inside parentheses, brackets, and braces.

**Book context:** Python treats all newlines as significant unless an explicit backslash is used at the end of a line to continue it to the next line.

**Linked from:** [Scanning / Design Note: Implicit Semicolons](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/scanning.html>)

### E041. automatic semicolon insertion

[Open resource](<https://www.ecma-international.org/ecma-262/5.1/#sec-7.9>)

**About:** The ECMAScript specification’s automatic semicolon insertion rules, contrasted with newline handling in other languages.

**Book context:** JavaScript’s “ automatic semicolon insertion ” rule is the real odd one.

**Linked from:** [Scanning / Design Note: Implicit Semicolons](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/scanning.html>)

### E042. Please Excuse My Dear Aunt Sally

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_operations#Mnemonics>)

**About:** Mnemonics for arithmetic precedence, used to motivate expression trees that preserve the order of operations.

**Book context:** Because you understand the order of operations — the old “ Please Excuse My Dear Aunt Sally ” stuff — you know that the multiplication is evaluated before the addition or subtraction.

**Linked from:** [Representing Code](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/representing-code.html>)

### E043. formal grammars

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formal_grammar>)

**About:** Formal production rules for generating a language’s valid strings, the foundation of the book’s syntax notation.

**Book context:** We need a bigger hammer, and that hammer is a context-free grammar ( CFG ). It’s the next heaviest tool in the toolbox of formal grammars .

**Linked from:** [Representing Code / 5 . 1 Context-Free Grammars](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/representing-code.html>)

### E044. unrestricted grammars

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unrestricted_grammar>)

**About:** Grammars whose production rules can replace sequences of symbols, more general than context-free grammars.

**Book context:** More powerful formalisms like unrestricted grammars allow a sequence of symbols in the head as well as in the body.

**Linked from:** [Representing Code / 5 . 1 . 1 Rules for grammars](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/representing-code.html>)

### E045. Backus-Naur form

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Backus%E2%80%93Naur_form>)

**About:** Backus–Naur form, a standard notation for writing context-free grammar productions.

**Book context:** Not much progress happened until John Backus and company needed a notation for specifying ALGOL 58 and came up with Backus-Naur form ( BNF ).

**Linked from:** [Representing Code / 5 . 1 . 1 Rules for grammars](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/representing-code.html>)

### E046. regular expressions

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regular_expression#Standards>)

**About:** Regular-expression notation and standards, cited to explain familiar repetition and optionality symbols in grammar rules.

**Book context:** If you’re used to grep or using regular expressions in your text editor, most of the punctuation should be familiar.

**Linked from:** [Representing Code / 5 . 1 . 2 Enhancing our notation](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/representing-code.html>)

### E047. EBNF

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extended_Backus%E2%80%93Naur_form>)

**About:** Extended Backus–Naur form, a grammar notation with convenient constructs for repetition, alternatives, and optional syntax.

**Book context:** As you work on programming languages, you’ll find that context-free grammars (using this or EBNF or some other notation) help you crystallize your informal syntax design ideas.

**Linked from:** [Representing Code / 5 . 1 . 2 Enhancing our notation](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/representing-code.html>)

### E048. Interpreter design pattern

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interpreter_pattern>)

**About:** The Interpreter design pattern, which represents syntax in objects that can evaluate or interpret themselves.

**Book context:** In effect, we could tell each syntax tree node, “Interpret thyself”. This is the Gang of Four’s Interpreter design pattern .

**Linked from:** [Representing Code / 5 . 3 Working with Trees](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/representing-code.html>); [Evaluating Expressions / 7 . 2 Evaluating Expressions](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/evaluating-expressions.html>)

### E049. separation of concerns

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separation_of_concerns>)

**About:** The design principle of keeping distinct responsibilities separate, used to motivate separating syntax nodes from operations on them.

**Book context:** If we added instance methods to the expression classes for every one of those operations, that would smush a bunch of different domains together. That violates separation of concerns and leads to hard-to-maintain code.

**Linked from:** [Representing Code / 5 . 3 Working with Trees](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/representing-code.html>)

### E050. reverse Polish notation

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reverse_Polish_notation>)

**About:** Postfix expression notation, where operators follow their operands and expressions can be evaluated with a stack.

**Book context:** In reverse Polish notation (RPN), the operands to an arithmetic operator are both placed before the operator, so 1 + 2 becomes 1 2 + .

**Linked from:** [Representing Code / Challenges](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/representing-code.html>)

### E051. IEEE 754

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Double-precision_floating-point_format>)

**About:** IEEE 754 binary64 floating-point representation, explaining precision limits and rounding effects in numeric evaluation.

**Book context:** In languages like Lox that use IEEE 754 double-precision floating-point numbers, the first evaluates to 0.006 , while the second yields 0.006000000000000001 .

**Linked from:** [Parsing Expressions / 6 . 1 Ambiguity and the Parsing Game](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/parsing-expressions.html>)

### E052. This

[Open resource](<https://docs.oracle.com/cd/E19957-01/806-3568/ncg_goldberg.html>)

**About:** David Goldberg’s What Every Computer Scientist Should Know About Floating-Point Arithmetic, covering representation, rounding, and numerical pitfalls.

**Book context:** Sometimes that tiny difference matters. This is a good place to learn more.

**Linked from:** [Parsing Expressions / 6 . 1 Ambiguity and the Parsing Game](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/parsing-expressions.html>)

### E053. LL(k)

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LL_parser>)

**About:** Top-down LL parsing, which reads left to right and constructs a leftmost derivation using lookahead.

**Book context:** There is a whole pack of parsing techniques whose names are mostly combinations of “L” and “R” — LL(k) , LR(1) , LALR — along with more exotic beasts like parser combinators , Earley parsers , the shunting yard algorithm , and packrat parsing .

**Linked from:** [Parsing Expressions / 6 . 2 Recursive Descent Parsing](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/parsing-expressions.html>)

### E054. LR(1)

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LR_parser>)

**About:** Bottom-up LR parsing, which reads left to right and reconstructs a rightmost derivation in reverse.

**Book context:** There is a whole pack of parsing techniques whose names are mostly combinations of “L” and “R” — LL(k) , LR(1) , LALR — along with more exotic beasts like parser combinators , Earley parsers , the shunting yard algorithm , and packrat parsing .

**Linked from:** [Parsing Expressions / 6 . 2 Recursive Descent Parsing](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/parsing-expressions.html>)

### E055. LALR

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LALR_parser>)

**About:** Look-ahead LR parsing, a compact LR-family technique widely used by parser generators.

**Book context:** There is a whole pack of parsing techniques whose names are mostly combinations of “L” and “R” — LL(k) , LR(1) , LALR — along with more exotic beasts like parser combinators , Earley parsers , the shunting yard algorithm , and packrat parsing .

**Linked from:** [Parsing Expressions / 6 . 2 Recursive Descent Parsing](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/parsing-expressions.html>)

### E056. parser combinators

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parser_combinator>)

**About:** A parsing approach that builds complex parsers by composing smaller parsing functions.

**Book context:** There is a whole pack of parsing techniques whose names are mostly combinations of “L” and “R” — LL(k) , LR(1) , LALR — along with more exotic beasts like parser combinators , Earley parsers , the shunting yard algorithm , and packrat parsing .

**Linked from:** [Parsing Expressions / 6 . 2 Recursive Descent Parsing](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/parsing-expressions.html>)

### E057. Earley parsers

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earley_parser>)

**About:** A general context-free parsing algorithm that handles a wide range of grammars using chart-based state tracking.

**Book context:** There is a whole pack of parsing techniques whose names are mostly combinations of “L” and “R” — LL(k) , LR(1) , LALR — along with more exotic beasts like parser combinators , Earley parsers , the shunting yard algorithm , and packrat parsing .

**Linked from:** [Parsing Expressions / 6 . 2 Recursive Descent Parsing](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/parsing-expressions.html>)

### E058. the shunting yard algorithm

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shunting-yard_algorithm>)

**About:** Dijkstra’s stack-based algorithm for handling expression precedence and associativity, often producing postfix output.

**Book context:** There is a whole pack of parsing techniques whose names are mostly combinations of “L” and “R” — LL(k) , LR(1) , LALR — along with more exotic beasts like parser combinators , Earley parsers , the shunting yard algorithm , and packrat parsing .

**Linked from:** [Parsing Expressions / 6 . 2 Recursive Descent Parsing](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/parsing-expressions.html>)

### E059. packrat parsing

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parsing_expression_grammar>)

**About:** Parsing expression grammars and packrat parsing: recognition-oriented grammar rules and memoization-based parsing techniques.

**Book context:** There is a whole pack of parsing techniques whose names are mostly combinations of “L” and “R” — LL(k) , LR(1) , LALR — along with more exotic beasts like parser combinators , Earley parsers , the shunting yard algorithm , and packrat parsing .

**Linked from:** [Parsing Expressions / 6 . 2 Recursive Descent Parsing](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/parsing-expressions.html>)

### E060. comma operator

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comma_operator>)

**About:** C’s comma operator, which evaluates a sequence of expressions and produces the final expression’s value.

**Book context:** The comma operator is an analogous syntax for expressions.

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IEEE_754>)

**About:** The floating-point arithmetic standard, including NaN behavior, infinities, rounding, and comparisons.

**Book context:** According to IEEE 754 , which specifies the behavior of double-precision numbers, dividing a zero by zero gives you the special NaN (“not a number”) value.

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Covariance_and_contravariance_(computer_science)#Covariant_arrays_in_Java_and_C.23>)

**About:** Subtyping variance, specifically Java and C# array covariance and the runtime checks needed when static array typing permits unsafe writes.

**Book context:** A more subtle example is covariant arrays in Java and C#.

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Value_(computer_science)#lrvalue>)

[Open resource](<http://clojuredocs.org/clojure.core/binding>)

**About:** Clojure’s binding macro, which temporarily rebinds dynamic variables within a dynamic extent.

### E065. with statement

[Open resource](<https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/JavaScript/Reference/Statements/with>)

**About:** JavaScript’s with statement, which alters name lookup by introducing an object’s properties into the lookup environment.

**Book context:** The widely disliked with statement in JavaScript turns properties on an object into dynamically scoped variables.

**Linked from:** [Statements and State / 8 . 5 Scope](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/statements-and-state.html>)

### E066. parent-pointer tree

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parent_pointer_tree>)

**About:** A tree represented by links from children to parents, used to explain nested environment chains and cactus stacks.

**Book context:** The boring name for this is a parent-pointer tree , but I much prefer the evocative cactus stack .

### E067. an option to enable or disable implicit variables

[Open resource](<https://msdn.microsoft.com/en-us/library/xe53dz5w(v=vs.100).aspx>)

**About:** Visual Basic’s Option Explicit setting, controlling whether variables must be explicitly declared rather than introduced implicitly.

**Book context:** Visual Basic has an option to enable or disable implicit variables .

### E068. crisis

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foundations_of_mathematics#Foundational_crisis>)

**About:** The foundational crisis in mathematics caused by paradoxes and questions about the consistency and limits of formal reasoning.

**Book context:** To address that crisis , they went back to square one.

**Linked from:** [Control Flow / 9 . 1 Turing Machines (Briefly)](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/control-flow.html>)

### E069. Russell’s paradox

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russell%27s_paradox>)

**About:** The contradiction arising from the set of all sets that do not contain themselves, motivating restrictions in formal set theory.

**Book context:** The most famous is Russell’s paradox .

**Linked from:** [Control Flow / 9 . 1 Turing Machines (Briefly)](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/control-flow.html>)

### E070. compute

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computable_function>)

**About:** Functions whose results can be obtained by an effective algorithm, central to defining the power and limits of computation.

**Book context:** They wanted to rigorously answer questions like, “Can all true statements be proven?”, “Can we compute all functions that we can define?”, or even the more general question, “What do we mean when we claim a function is ‘computable’?”

**Linked from:** [Control Flow / 9 . 1 Turing Machines (Briefly)](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/control-flow.html>)

### E071. equivalent in power

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church%E2%80%93Turing_thesis>)

**About:** The thesis relating effectively computable functions to formal models such as Turing machines and lambda calculus.

**Book context:** Turing machines have better name recognition — there’s no Hollywood film about Alonzo Church yet — but the two formalisms are equivalent in power .

**Linked from:** [Control Flow / 9 . 1 Turing Machines (Briefly)](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/control-flow.html>)

### E072. dangling else

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dangling_else>)

**About:** The grammar ambiguity in nested conditional statements about which unmatched if receives an else clause.

**Book context:** Since else clauses are optional, and there is no explicit delimiter marking the end of the if statement, the grammar is ambiguous when you nest if s in this way. This classic pitfall of syntax is called the dangling else problem.

**Linked from:** [Control Flow / 9 . 2 Conditional Execution](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/control-flow.html>)

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiple_dispatch>)

**About:** Selecting a method using the runtime types of multiple arguments, rather than only one receiver.

### E108. deterministic finite automaton

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deterministic_finite_automaton>)

**About:** A finite-state recognizer with a single determined transition for each state and input symbol, related to keyword recognition and tries.

**Book context:** Tries are a special case of an even more fundamental data structure: a deterministic finite automaton ( DFA ).

**Linked from:** [Scanning on Demand / 16 . 4 . 1 Tries and state machines](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/scanning-on-demand.html>)

### E109. game programming

[Open resource](<http://gameprogrammingpatterns.com/state.html>)

**About:** The State chapter of Game Programming Patterns, explaining finite-state machines and state-based behavior.

**Book context:** State machines are rad. They end up useful in everything from game programming to implementing networking protocols.

**Linked from:** [Scanning on Demand / 16 . 4 . 1 Tries and state machines](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/scanning-on-demand.html>)

### E110. syntax diagram

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syntax_diagram>)

**About:** Railroad or syntax diagrams, a visual notation showing the paths allowed by a grammar.

**Book context:** This style of diagram is called a syntax diagram or the more charming railroad diagram .

**Linked from:** [Scanning on Demand / 16 . 4 . 1 Tries and state machines](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/scanning-on-demand.html>)

### E111. essentially what V8 does

[Open resource](<https://github.com/v8/v8/blob/e77eebfe3b747fb315bd3baad09bec0953e53e68/src/parsing/scanner.cc#L1643>)

**About:** A specific version of V8’s scanner source code, cited as a production example of direct keyword recognition.

**Book context:** The same approach is essentially what V8 does , and that’s currently one of the world’s most sophisticated, fastest language implementations.

**Linked from:** [Scanning on Demand / 16 . 4 . 1 Tries and state machines](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/scanning-on-demand.html>)

### E112. string interpolation

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/String_interpolation>)

**About:** Embedding expressions inside a string literal and evaluating them to construct the resulting string.

**Book context:** Many newer languages support string interpolation .

**Linked from:** [Scanning on Demand / Challenges](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/scanning-on-demand.html>)

### E113. “Pratt Parsing: Expression Parsing Made Easy”

[Open resource](<http://journal.stuffwithstuff.com/2011/03/19/pratt-parsers-expression-parsing-made-easy/>)

**About:** Robert Nystrom’s Pratt parsing tutorial, presenting expression parsing with binding power in a Java/object-oriented style.

**Book context:** If this chapter isn’t clicking with you and you’d like another take on the concepts, I wrote an article that teaches the same algorithm but using Java and an object-oriented style: “Pratt Parsing: Expression Parsing Made Easy” .

**Linked from:** [Compiling Expressions](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/compiling-expressions.html>)

### E114. The C Programming Language

[Open resource](<https://www.cs.princeton.edu/~bwk/cbook.html>)

**About:** The C Programming Language by Brian Kernighan and Dennis Ritchie, recommended as an introductory C reference.

**Book context:** If you are looking for a C tutorial, I love The C Programming Language , usually called “K&R” in honor of its authors.

**Linked from:** [Types of Values / 18 . 3 . 1 Unary negation and runtime errors](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/types-of-values.html>)

### E115. type punning

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_punning>)

**About:** Interpreting stored bits through different types, discussed when representing dynamically typed values in C.

**Book context:** It’s been around for ages, to the point that the C specification carves out specific support for it, but I don’t know that it has a canonical name. It’s an example of type punning , but that term is too broad.

**Linked from:** [Strings / 19 . 2 Struct Inheritance](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/strings.html>)

### E116. flexible array members

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flexible_array_member>)

**About:** A C struct’s trailing array of runtime-selected size, suggested for storing a string’s characters with its object header in one allocation.

**Book context:** A more efficient solution relies on a technique called flexible array members .

**Linked from:** [Strings / Challenges](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/strings.html>)

### E117. ASCII

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ASCII>)

**About:** The ASCII character-encoding standard, used as historical background for string representation and character sets.

**Book context:** The first widely adopted standard answer to this was ASCII .

**Linked from:** [Strings / Design Note: String Encoding](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/strings.html>)

### E118. Unicode

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unicode>)

**About:** The Unicode standard for representing characters from many writing systems through code points and related text-processing conventions.

**Book context:** Next came Unicode .

**Linked from:** [Strings / Design Note: String Encoding](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/strings.html>)

### E119. Wren

[Open resource](<http://wren.io>)

**About:** Wren, Robert Nystrom’s small class-based scripting language, cited in discussions of object models and UTF-8 string design.

**Book context:** For my little embedded scripting language Wren , I went with UTF-8 and code points.

**Linked from:** [Strings / Design Note: String Encoding](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/strings.html>)

### E120. birthday paradox

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birthday_problem>)

**About:** The surprisingly rapid growth in collision probability as more values are sampled, used to motivate hash-collision handling.

**Book context:** The birthday paradox tells us that as the number of entries in the hash table increases, the chance of collision increases very quickly.

**Linked from:** [Hash Tables / 20 . 2 Collision Resolution](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/hash-tables.html>)

### E121. pigeonhole principle

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pigeonhole_principle>)

**About:** The counting principle that more items than containers force at least one shared container, explaining unavoidable hash collisions.

**Book context:** A low load factor can make collisions rarer , but the pigeonhole principle tells us we can never eliminate them entirely.

**Linked from:** [Hash Tables / 20 . 2 Collision Resolution](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/hash-tables.html>)

### E122. FNV-1a

[Open resource](<http://www.isthe.com/chongo/tech/comp/fnv/>)

**About:** The Fowler–Noll–Vo family of hash functions, including the FNV-1a variant selected for clox string hashing.

**Book context:** So for clox, I picked a simple, well-worn hash function called FNV-1a that’s served me fine over the years.

**Linked from:** [Hash Tables / 20 . 3 Hash Functions](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/hash-tables.html>)

### E123. structured programming

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Structured_programming>)

**About:** Programming with structured control constructs such as sequences, conditionals, and loops rather than unrestricted jumps.

**Book context:** Lox is a structured programming language, but clox bytecode isn’t.

**Linked from:** [Jumping Back and Forth](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/jumping-back-and-forth.html>)

### E124. proved

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Structured_program_theorem>)

**About:** The Böhm–Jacopini result that structured sequencing, selection, and iteration can express computations otherwise written using arbitrary jumps.

**Book context:** Böhm and Jacopini proved that any control flow using goto can be transformed into one using just sequencing, loops, and branches.

**Linked from:** [Jumping Back and Forth / Design Note: Considering Goto Harmful](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/jumping-back-and-forth.html>)

### E125. continuations

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continuation>)

**About:** A representation of the remainder of a computation, explaining why some languages cannot implement every call using a simple conventional stack.

**Book context:** Many Lisp implementations dynamically allocate stack frames because it simplifies implementing continuations .

**Linked from:** [Calls and Functions / 24 . 3 . 3 The call stack](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/calls-and-functions.html>)

### E126. inverted pyramid

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inverted_pyramid_(journalism)>)

**About:** A writing structure that puts the most important information first, used as an analogy for ordering stack traces.

**Book context:** On the other hand, the “ inverted pyramid ” from journalism tells us we should put the most important information first in a block of text.

**Linked from:** [Calls and Functions / 24 . 5 . 3 Printing stack traces](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/calls-and-functions.html>)

### E127. map()

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Map_(higher-order_function)>)

**About:** A higher-order operation that applies a function to every element in a collection, illustrating frequent closure calls.

**Book context:** Think about the closures passed to typical higher-order functions on collections like map() and filter() .

**Linked from:** [Closures / 25 . 4 . 3 Tracking open upvalues](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/closures.html>)

### E128. filter()

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filter_(higher-order_function)>)

**About:** A higher-order operation that retains elements satisfying a predicate, another common use of closures.

**Book context:** Think about the closures passed to typical higher-order functions on collections like map() and filter() .

**Linked from:** [Closures / 25 . 4 . 3 Tracking open upvalues](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/closures.html>)

### E129. famous koan

[Open resource](<http://wiki.c2.com/?ClosuresAndObjectsAreEquivalent>)

**About:** The well-known comparison between objects and closures, motivating an exercise that emulates object-like state with closures.

**Book context:** A famous koan teaches us that “objects are a poor man’s closure” (and vice versa).

**Linked from:** [Closures / Challenges](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/closures.html>)

### E130. The Garbage Collection Handbook

[Open resource](<http://gchandbook.org/>)

**About:** The Garbage Collection Handbook, a comprehensive reference on automatic memory-management algorithms.

**Book context:** If you want to explore other GC algorithms, The Garbage Collection Handbook (Jones, et al.) is the canonical reference.

**Linked from:** [Garbage Collection / 26 . 1 Reachability](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/garbage-collection.html>)

### E131. weak reference

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weak_reference>)

**About:** A reference that does not keep its target alive for garbage collection, used for the interned-string table.

**Book context:** When that happens, the dangling reference must be fixed too, sort of like a magic, self-clearing pointer. This particular set of semantics comes up frequently enough that it has a name: a weak reference .

**Linked from:** [Garbage Collection / 26 . 5 . 1 Weak references and the string pool](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/garbage-collection.html>)

### E132. Boehm–Demers–Weiser garbage collector

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boehm_garbage_collector>)

**About:** The Boehm–Demers–Weiser conservative garbage collector, which can discover potential pointers in native memory and stacks.

**Book context:** The most well-known of this variety is the Boehm–Demers–Weiser garbage collector , usually just called the “Boehm collector”.

**Linked from:** [Garbage Collection / 26 . 7 Garbage Collection Bugs](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/garbage-collection.html>)

### E133. Monte Carlo algorithms

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monte_Carlo_algorithm>)

**About:** Randomized algorithms that trade a bounded chance of an incorrect result for useful performance guarantees.

**Book context:** There are cases where users may be satisfied when a program sometimes returns the wrong answer in return for running significantly faster or with a better bound on the performance. These are the field of Monte Carlo algorithms .

**Linked from:** [Methods and Initializers / 28 . 5 . 1 Invoking fields](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/methods-and-initializers.html>)

### E134. strangeness budget

[Open resource](<https://words.steveklabnik.com/the-language-strangeness-budget>)

**About:** Steve Klabnik’s language-design essay arguing that learners tolerate only a limited amount of unfamiliar syntax and behavior.

**Book context:** I think of this balancing act in terms of a novelty budget , or as Steve Klabnik calls it, a “ strangeness budget ”.

**Linked from:** [Methods and Initializers / Design Note: Novelty Budget](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/methods-and-initializers.html>)

### E135. idiosyncrasy credit

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idiosyncrasy_credit>)

**About:** The social-psychology concept of earning tolerance for nonconforming behavior through prior conformity, used as an analogy for language novelty.

**Book context:** A related concept in psychology is idiosyncrasy credit , the idea that other people in society grant you a finite amount of deviations from social norms.

**Linked from:** [Methods and Initializers / Design Note: Novelty Budget](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/methods-and-initializers.html>)

### E136. MIT license

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MIT_License>)

**About:** The permissive MIT software license used for the book’s interpreter source code, distinct from the license on most book prose and illustrations.

**Book context:** The text of this book is copyrighted to me, but the code and the implementations of jlox and clox use the very permissive MIT license .

**Linked from:** [Optimization / 30 . 4 Where to Next](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/optimization.html>)

### E137. context free

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Context-free_grammar>)

**About:** A grammar in which each production replaces one nonterminal independently of its surrounding symbols, used for Lox syntax.

**Book context:** Where the syntax is context free , the lexical grammar is regular — note that there are no recursive rules.

**Linked from:** [Appendix I / A1 . 2 Lexical Grammar](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/appendix-i.html>)

### E138. regular

[Open resource](<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regular_grammar>)

**About:** A restricted grammar corresponding to regular languages and finite-state recognition, contrasted with recursive syntax grammars.

**Book context:** Where the syntax is context free , the lexical grammar is regular — note that there are no recursive rules.

**Linked from:** [Appendix I / A1 . 2 Lexical Grammar](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/appendix-i.html>)

### B016. Design Note: Static and Dynamic Typing

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/evaluating-expressions.html#design-note>)

**About:** Section: Design Note: Static and Dynamic Typing. Some languages, like Java, are statically typed which means type errors are detected and reported at compile time before any code is run. Others, like Lox, are dynamically typed and defer checking for type errors until runtime right before an operation is attempted. ### B044. Design Note: Closing Over the Loop Variable

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/closures.html#design-note>)

**About:** Section: Design Note: Closing Over the Loop Variable. Closures capture variables. When two closures capture the same variable, they share a reference to the same underlying storage location. This fact is visible when new values are assigned to the variable. Obviously, if two closures capture different variables, there is no sharing.

### B046. Design Note: Generational Collectors

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/garbage-collection.html#design-note>)

**About:** Section: Design Note: Generational Collectors. A collector loses throughput if it spends a long time re-visiting objects that are still alive. But it can increase latency if it avoids collecting and accumulates a large pile of garbage to wade through. If only there were some way to tell which objects were likely to be long-lived and which weren’t. Then the GC could avoid revisiting the long-lived ones…

### B055. 1 . 1 Why Learn This Stuff?

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html#why-learn-this-stuff>)

**About:** Section: 1 . 1 Why Learn This Stuff?. Every introduction to every compiler book seems to have this section. I don’t know what it is about programming languages that causes such existential doubt. I don’t think ornithology books worry about justifying their existence. They assume the reader loves birds and start teaching.

**Linked from:** [Introduction / Navigation](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html>); [Introduction / 1 . 1 Why Learn This Stuff?](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html>)

### B056. 1 . 2 How the Book Is Organized

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html#how-the-book-is-organized>)

**About:** Section: 1 . 2 How the Book Is Organized. This book is broken into three parts. You’re reading the first one now. It’s a couple of chapters to get you oriented, teach you some of the lingo that language hackers use, and introduce you to Lox, the language we’ll be implementing.

**Linked from:** [Introduction / Navigation](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html>); [Introduction / 1 . 2 How the Book Is Organized](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html>)

### B057. 1 . 3 The First Interpreter

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html#the-first-interpreter>)

**About:** Section: 1 . 3 The First Interpreter. We’ll write our first interpreter, jlox, in Java . The focus is on concepts . We’ll write the simplest, cleanest code we can to correctly implement the semantics of the language. This will get us comfortable with the basic techniques and also hone our understanding of exactly how the language is supposed to behave.

**Linked from:** [Introduction / Navigation](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html>); [Introduction / 1 . 3 The First Interpreter](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html>)

### B058. 1 . 4 The Second Interpreter

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html#the-second-interpreter>)

**About:** Section: 1 . 4 The Second Interpreter. So in the next part, we start all over again, but this time in C. C is the perfect language for understanding how an implementation really works, all the way down to the bytes in memory and the code flowing through the CPU.

**Linked from:** [Introduction / Navigation](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html>); [Introduction / 1 . 4 The Second Interpreter](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html>)

### B059. Challenges

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html#challenges>)

**About:** Section: Challenges. There are at least six domain-specific languages used in the little system I cobbled together to write and publish this book. What are they? Get a “Hello, world!” program written and running in Java. Set up whatever makefiles or IDE projects you need to get it working. If you have a debugger, get comfortable with it and step through your program as it runs.…

**Linked from:** [Introduction / Navigation](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html>); [Introduction / Challenges](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html>)

### B060. 1 . 1 . 1 Little languages are everywhere

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html#little-languages-are-everywhere>)

**About:** Section: 1 . 1 . 1 Little languages are everywhere. For every successful general-purpose language, there are a thousand successful niche ones. We used to call them “little languages”, but inflation in the jargon economy led to the name “domain-specific languages”. These are pidgins tailor-built to a specific task. Think application scripting languages, template engines, markup formats, and configuration…

**Linked from:** [Introduction / 1 . 1 . 1 Little languages are everywhere](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html>)

### B061. 1 . 1 . 2 Languages are great exercise

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html#languages-are-great-exercise>)

**About:** Section: 1 . 1 . 2 Languages are great exercise. Long distance runners sometimes train with weights strapped to their ankles or at high altitudes where the atmosphere is thin. When they later unburden themselves, the new relative ease of light limbs and oxygen-rich air enables them to run farther and faster.

**Linked from:** [Introduction / 1 . 1 . 2 Languages are great exercise](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html>)

### B062. 1 . 1 . 3 One more reason

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html#one-more-reason>)

**About:** Section: 1 . 1 . 3 One more reason. This last reason is hard for me to admit, because it’s so close to my heart. Ever since I learned to program as a kid, I felt there was something magical about languages. When I first tapped out BASIC programs one key at a time I couldn’t conceive how BASIC itself was made.

**Linked from:** [Introduction / 1 . 1 . 3 One more reason](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html>)

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html#the-code>)

**About:** Section: 1 . 2 . 1 The code. We’re about crafting interpreters, so this book contains real code. Every single line of code needed is included, and each snippet tells you where to insert it in your ever-growing implementation.

**Linked from:** [Introduction / 1 . 2 . 1 The code](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html>)

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html#snippets>)

Also, because I try to keep the program in a runnable state even when major features are missing, sometimes we add temporary code that gets replaced in later snippets.

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html#asides>)

There’s nothing that you need to know in them to understand later parts of the book, so you can skip them if you want. I won’t judge you, but I might be a little sad.

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html#challenges_>)

**About:** Section: 1 . 2 . 4 Challenges. Each chapter ends with a few exercises. Unlike textbook problem sets, which tend to review material you already covered, these are to help you learn more than what’s in the chapter. They force you to step off the guided path and explore on your own. They will make you research other languages, figure out how to implement features, or otherwise get you out…

**Linked from:** [Introduction / 1 . 2 . 4 Challenges](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html>)

### B067. 1 . 2 . 5 Design notes

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html#design-notes>)

**About:** Section: 1 . 2 . 5 Design notes. Most “programming language” books are strictly programming language implementation books. They rarely discuss how one might happen to design the language being implemented. Implementation is fun because it is so precisely defined . We programmers seem to have an affinity for things that are black and white, ones and zeroes.

**Linked from:** [Introduction / 1 . 2 . 5 Design notes](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/introduction.html>)

### B068. 2 . 1 The Parts of a Language

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html#the-parts-of-a-language>)

**About:** Section: 2 . 1 The Parts of a Language. Engineers have been building programming languages since the Dark Ages of computing. As soon as we could talk to computers, we discovered doing so was too hard, and we enlisted their help. I find it fascinating that even though today’s machines are literally a million times faster and have orders of magnitude more storage, the way we build programming…

**Linked from:** [A Map of the Territory / Navigation](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>); [A Map of the Territory / 2 . 1 The Parts of a Language](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>)

### B069. 2 . 2 Shortcuts and Alternate Routes

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html#shortcuts-and-alternate-routes>)

**About:** Section: 2 . 2 Shortcuts and Alternate Routes. That’s the long path covering every possible phase you might implement. Many languages do walk the entire route, but there are a few shortcuts and alternate paths.

**Linked from:** [A Map of the Territory / Navigation](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>); [A Map of the Territory / 2 . 2 Shortcuts and Alternate Routes](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>)

### B070. 2 . 3 Compilers and Interpreters

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html#compilers-and-interpreters>)

**About:** Section: 2 . 3 Compilers and Interpreters. Now that I’ve stuffed your head with a dictionary’s worth of programming language jargon, we can finally address a question that’s plagued coders since time immemorial: What’s the difference between a compiler and an interpreter?

**Linked from:** [A Map of the Territory / Navigation](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>); [A Map of the Territory / 2 . 3 Compilers and Interpreters](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>)

### B071. 2 . 4 Our Journey

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html#our-journey>)

**About:** Section: 2 . 4 Our Journey. That’s a lot to take in all at once. Don’t worry. This isn’t the chapter where you’re expected to understand all of these pieces and parts. I just want you to know that they are out there and roughly how they fit together.

**Linked from:** [A Map of the Territory / Navigation](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>); [A Map of the Territory / 2 . 4 Our Journey](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>)

### B072. Challenges

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html#challenges>)

**About:** Section: Challenges. Pick an open source implementation of a language you like. Download the source code and poke around in it. Try to find the code that implements the scanner and parser. Are they handwritten, or generated using tools like Lex and Yacc? ( .l or .y files usually imply the latter.) Just-in-time compilation tends to be the fastest way to implement dynamically…

**Linked from:** [A Map of the Territory / Navigation](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>); [A Map of the Territory / Challenges](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>)

### B073. 2 . 1 . 1 Scanning

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html#scanning>)

**About:** Section: 2 . 1 . 1 Scanning. The first step is scanning , also known as lexing , or (if you’re trying to impress someone) lexical analysis . They all mean pretty much the same thing. I like “lexing” because it sounds like something an evil supervillain would do, but I’ll use “scanning” because it seems to be marginally more commonplace.

**Linked from:** [A Map of the Territory / 2 . 1 . 1 Scanning](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>)

### B074. 2 . 1 . 2 Parsing

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html#parsing>)

**About:** Section: 2 . 1 . 2 Parsing. The next step is parsing . This is where our syntax gets a grammar — the ability to compose larger expressions and statements out of smaller parts. Did you ever diagram sentences in English class? If so, you’ve done what a parser does, except that English has thousands and thousands of “keywords” and an overflowing cornucopia of ambiguity. Programming…

**Linked from:** [A Map of the Territory / 2 . 1 . 2 Parsing](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>)

### B075. 2 . 1 . 3 Static analysis

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html#static-analysis>)

**About:** Section: 2 . 1 . 3 Static analysis. The first two stages are pretty similar across all implementations. Now, the individual characteristics of each language start coming into play. At this point, we know the syntactic structure of the code — things like which expressions are nested in which — but we don’t know much more than that.

**Linked from:** [A Map of the Territory / 2 . 1 . 3 Static analysis](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>)

### B076. 2 . 1 . 4 Intermediate representations

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html#intermediate-representations>)

**About:** Section: 2 . 1 . 4 Intermediate representations. You can think of the compiler as a pipeline where each stage’s job is to organize the data representing the user’s code in a way that makes the next stage simpler to implement. The front end of the pipeline is specific to the source language the program is written in. The back end is concerned with the final architecture where the program will run.

**Linked from:** [A Map of the Territory / 2 . 1 . 4 Intermediate representations](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>)

### B077. 2 . 1 . 5 Optimization

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html#optimization>)

**About:** Section: 2 . 1 . 5 Optimization. Once we understand what the user’s program means, we are free to swap it out with a different program that has the same semantics but implements them more efficiently — we can optimize it.

**Linked from:** [A Map of the Territory / 2 . 1 . 5 Optimization](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>)

### B078. 2 . 1 . 6 Code generation

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html#code-generation>)

**About:** Section: 2 . 1 . 6 Code generation. We have applied all of the optimizations we can think of to the user’s program. The last step is converting it to a form the machine can actually run. In other words, generating code (or code gen ), where “code” here usually refers to the kind of primitive assembly-like instructions a CPU runs and not the kind of “source code” a human might want to read.

**Linked from:** [A Map of the Territory / 2 . 1 . 6 Code generation](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>)

### B079. 2 . 1 . 7 Virtual machine

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html#virtual-machine>)

**About:** Section: 2 . 1 . 7 Virtual machine. If your compiler produces bytecode, your work isn’t over once that’s done. Since there is no chip that speaks that bytecode, it’s your job to translate. Again, you have two options. You can write a little mini-compiler for each target architecture that converts the bytecode to native code for that machine. You still have to do work for each chip you…

**Linked from:** [A Map of the Territory / 2 . 1 . 7 Virtual machine](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>)

### B080. 2 . 1 . 8 Runtime

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html#runtime>)

**About:** Section: 2 . 1 . 8 Runtime. We have finally hammered the user’s program into a form that we can execute. The last step is running it. If we compiled it to machine code, we simply tell the operating system to load the executable and off it goes. If we compiled it to bytecode, we need to start up the VM and load the program into that.

**Linked from:** [A Map of the Territory / 2 . 1 . 8 Runtime](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>)

### B081. 2 . 2 . 1 Single-pass compilers

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html#single-pass-compilers>)

**About:** Section: 2 . 2 . 1 Single-pass compilers. Some simple compilers interleave parsing, analysis, and code generation so that they produce output code directly in the parser, without ever allocating any syntax trees or other IRs. These single-pass compilers restrict the design of the language. You have no intermediate data structures to store global information about the program, and you don’t revisit…

**Linked from:** [A Map of the Territory / 2 . 2 . 1 Single-pass compilers](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>)

### B082. 2 . 2 . 2 Tree-walk interpreters

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html#tree-walk-interpreters>)

**About:** Section: 2 . 2 . 2 Tree-walk interpreters. Some programming languages begin executing code right after parsing it to an AST (with maybe a bit of static analysis applied). To run the program, the interpreter traverses the syntax tree one branch and leaf at a time, evaluating each node as it goes.

**Linked from:** [A Map of the Territory / 2 . 2 . 2 Tree-walk interpreters](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>)

### B083. 2 . 2 . 3 Transpilers

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html#transpilers>)

**About:** Section: 2 . 2 . 3 Transpilers. Writing a complete back end for a language can be a lot of work. If you have some existing generic IR to target, you could bolt your front end onto that. Otherwise, it seems like you’re stuck. But what if you treated some other source language as if it were an intermediate representation?

**Linked from:** [A Map of the Territory / 2 . 2 . 3 Transpilers](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>)

### B084. 2 . 2 . 4 Just-in-time compilation

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html#just-in-time-compilation>)

**About:** Section: 2 . 2 . 4 Just-in-time compilation. This last one is less a shortcut and more a dangerous alpine scramble best reserved for experts. The fastest way to execute code is by compiling it to machine code, but you might not know what architecture your end user’s machine supports. What to do?

**Linked from:** [A Map of the Territory / 2 . 2 . 4 Just-in-time compilation](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/a-map-of-the-territory.html>)

**Linked from:** [The Lox Language / Navigation](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html>); [The Lox Language / 3 . 4 Expressions](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html>)

### B089. 3 . 5 Statements

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html#statements>)

**About:** Section: 3 . 5 Statements. Now we’re at statements. Where an expression’s main job is to produce a value , a statement’s job is to produce an effect . Since, by definition, statements don’t evaluate to a value, to be useful they have to otherwise change the world in some way — usually modifying some state, reading input, or producing output.

**Linked from:** [The Lox Language / Navigation](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html>); [The Lox Language / 3 . 5 Statements](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html>)

### B090. 3 . 6 Variables

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html#variables>)

**About:** Section: 3 . 6 Variables. You declare variables using var statements. If you omit the initializer, the variable’s value defaults to nil .

**Linked from:** [The Lox Language / Navigation](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html>); [The Lox Language / 3 . 6 Variables](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html>)

### B091. 3 . 7 Control Flow

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html#control-flow>)

**About:** Section: 3 . 7 Control Flow. It’s hard to write useful programs if you can’t skip some code or execute some more than once. That means control flow. In addition to the logical operators we already covered, Lox lifts three statements straight from C.

**Linked from:** [The Lox Language / Navigation](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html>); [The Lox Language / 3 . 7 Control Flow](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html>)

### B092. 3 . 8 Functions

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html#functions>)

**About:** Section: 3 . 8 Functions. A function call expression looks the same as it does in C.

### B093. 3 . 9 Classes

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html#classes>)

**About:** Section: 3 . 9 Classes. Since Lox has dynamic typing, lexical (roughly, “block”) scope, and closures, it’s about halfway to being a functional language. But as you’ll see, it’s also about halfway to being an object-oriented language. Both paradigms have a lot going for them, so I thought it was worth covering some of each.

**Linked from:** [The Lox Language / Navigation](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html>); [The Lox Language / 3 . 9 Classes](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html>)

**About:** Section: 3 . 10 The Standard Library. We’re almost done. That’s the whole language, so all that’s left is the “core” or “standard” library — the set of functionality that is implemented directly in the interpreter and that all user-defined behavior is built on top of.

Does it do what you expect? Why or why not? This informal introduction leaves a lot unspecified. List several open questions you have about the language’s syntax and semantics. What do you…

### B096. 3 . 2 . 1 Dynamic typing

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html#dynamic-typing>)

**About:** Section: 3 . 2 . 1 Dynamic typing. Lox is dynamically typed. Variables can store values of any type, and a single variable can even store values of different types at different times. If you try to perform an operation on values of the wrong type — say, dividing a number by a string — then the error is detected and reported at runtime.

**Linked from:** [The Lox Language / 3 . 2 . 1 Dynamic typing](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html>)

### B097. 3 . 2 . 2 Automatic memory management

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html#automatic-memory-management>)

**About:** Section: 3 . 2 . 2 Automatic memory management. High-level languages exist to eliminate error-prone, low-level drudgery, and what could be more tedious than manually managing the allocation and freeing of storage? No one rises and greets the morning sun with, “I can’t wait to figure out the correct place to call free() for every byte of memory I allocate today!”

**Linked from:** [The Lox Language / 3 . 2 . 2 Automatic memory management](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html>)

(When we get to parsing, we’ll get way more precise about that.) In cases where the precedence isn’t what you want, you can use () to group stuff.

### B103. 3 . 9 . 1 Why might any language want to be object oriented?

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html#why-might-any-language-want-to-be-object-oriented>)

**About:** Section: 3 . 9 . 1 Why might any language want to be object oriented?. Now that object-oriented languages like Java have sold out and only play arena shows, it’s not cool to like them anymore. Why would anyone make a new language with objects? Isn’t that like releasing music on 8-track?

**Linked from:** [The Lox Language / 3 . 9 . 1 Why might any language want to be object oriented?](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html>)

### B104. 3 . 9 . 2 Why is Lox object oriented?

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html#why-is-lox-object-oriented>)

**About:** Section: 3 . 9 . 2 Why is Lox object oriented?. I could claim objects are groovy but still out of scope for the book. Most programming language books, especially ones that try to implement a whole language, leave objects out. To me, that means the topic isn’t well covered. With such a widespread paradigm, that omission makes me sad.

**Linked from:** [The Lox Language / 3 . 9 . 2 Why is Lox object oriented?](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html>)

### B105. 3 . 9 . 3 Classes or prototypes

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html#classes-or-prototypes>)

**About:** Section: 3 . 9 . 3 Classes or prototypes. When it comes to objects, there are actually two approaches to them, classes and prototypes . Classes came first, and are more common thanks to C++, Java, C#, and friends. Prototypes were a virtually forgotten offshoot until JavaScript accidentally took over the world.

**Linked from:** [The Lox Language / 3 . 9 . 3 Classes or prototypes](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html>)

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html#instantiation-and-initialization>)

**About:** Section: 3 . 9 . 5 Instantiation and initialization. Classes that only have behavior aren’t super useful. The idea behind object-oriented programming is encapsulating behavior and state together. To do that, you need fields. Lox, like other dynamically typed languages, lets you freely add properties onto objects.

**Linked from:** [The Lox Language / 3 . 9 . 5 Instantiation and initialization](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html>)

**About:** Section: 3 . 9 . 6 Inheritance. Every object-oriented language lets you not only define methods, but reuse them across multiple classes or objects. For that, Lox supports single inheritance. When you declare a class, you can specify a class that it inherits from using a less-than ( &lt; ) operator.

**Linked from:** [The Lox Language / 3 . 9 . 6 Inheritance](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/the-lox-language.html>)

### B111. 4 . 3 Regular Languages and Expressions

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/scanning.html#regular-languages-and-expressions>)

**About:** Section: 4 . 3 Regular Languages and Expressions. Now that we know what we’re trying to produce, let’s, well, produce it. The core of the scanner is a loop. Starting at the first character of the source code, the scanner figures out what lexeme the character belongs to, and consumes it and any following characters that are part of that lexeme. When it reaches the end of that lexeme, it emits a token.

**Linked from:** [Scanning / Navigation](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/scanning.html>); [Scanning / 4 . 3 Regular Languages and Expressions](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/scanning.html>)

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/scanning.html#recognizing-lexemes>)

Imagine if every lexeme were only a single character long. All you would need to do is consume the next character and pick a token type for it. Several lexemes are only a single character in Lox, so let’s start with those.

You might think we could match keywords like or in the same way we handle multiple-character operators like &lt;= .

Aside from separating tokens — distinguishing print foo from printfoo — spaces aren’t used for much in most languages. However, in a couple of dark corners, a space does affect how code is parsed in CoffeeScript, Ruby, and the C preprocessor. Where and…

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/scanning.html#error-handling>)

But if you care about making a language that’s actually usable , then handling errors gracefully is vital.

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/scanning.html#token-type>)

Keywords are part of the shape of the language’s grammar, so the parser often has code like, “If the next token is while then do . . . ” That means the parser wants to know not just that it has a lexeme for some identifier, but that it has a reserved word, and which keyword it is.

Since the scanner has to walk each character in the literal to correctly identify it, it can also convert that textual representation of a value to the living runtime object that will be used by the interpreter later.

In our simple interpreter, we note only which line the token appears on, but more sophisticated implementations include the column and length too.

They aren’t used by the Lox language, but that doesn’t mean the interpreter can pretend they…

You can’t write ! = in Lox and have it behave like an…

### B125. 5 . 1 Context-Free Grammars

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/representing-code.html#context-free-grammars>)

**About:** Section: 5 . 1 Context-Free Grammars. In the last chapter, the formalism we used for defining the lexical grammar — the rules for how characters get grouped into tokens — was called a regular language . That was fine for our scanner, which emits a flat sequence of tokens. But regular languages aren’t powerful enough to handle expressions which can nest arbitrarily deeply.

**About:** Section: 5 . 2 Implementing Syntax Trees. Finally, we get to write some code. That little expression grammar is our skeleton. Since the grammar is recursive — note how grouping , unary , and binary all refer back to expression — our data structure will form a tree. Since this structure represents the syntax of our language, it’s called a syntax tree .

**Linked from:** [Representing Code / Navigation](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/representing-code.html>); [Representing Code / 5 . 2 Implementing Syntax Trees](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/representing-code.html>)

### B129. Challenges

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/representing-code.html#challenges>)

**About:** Section: Challenges. Earlier, I said that the | , \* , and + forms we added to our grammar metasyntax were just syntactic sugar. Take this grammar: expr → expr ( "(" ( expr ( "," expr )\* )? ")" | "." IDENTIFIER )+ | IDENTIFIER | NUMBER Produce a grammar that matches the same language but does not use any of that notational sugar. Bonus: What kind of expression does this bit of…

**About:** Section: 6 . 3 . 3 Synchronizing a recursive descent parser. With recursive descent, the parser’s state — which rules it is in the middle of recognizing — is not stored explicitly in fields. Instead, we use Java’s own call stack to track what the parser is doing. Each rule in the middle of being parsed is a call frame on the stack. In order to reset that state, we need to clear out those call frames.

**About:** Section: 20 . 2 . 1 Separate chaining. Techniques for resolving collisions fall into two broad categories. The first is separate chaining . Instead of each bucket containing a single entry, we let it contain a collection of them. In the classic implementation, each bucket points to a linked list of entries. To look up an entry, you find its bucket and then walk the list until you find an entry…

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/hash-tables.html#open-addressing>)

**About:** Section: 20 . 2 . 2 Open addressing. The other technique is called open addressing or (confusingly) closed hashing . With this technique, all entries live directly in the bucket array, with one entry per bucket. If two entries collide in the same bucket, we find a different empty bucket to use instead.

### B369. 24 . 3 . 2 Return addresses

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/calls-and-functions.html#return-addresses>)

**About:** Section: 24 . 3 . 2 Return addresses. Right now, the VM works its way through the instruction stream by incrementing the ip field. The only interesting behavior is around control flow instructions which offset the ip by larger amounts. Calling a function is pretty straightforward — simply set ip to point to the first instruction in that function’s chunk. But what about when the function is done?

[Open original book location](<https://craftinginterpreters.com/garbage-collection.html#mark-sweep-garbage-collection>)

**About:** Section: 26 . 2 Mark-Sweep Garbage Collection. The first managed language was Lisp, the second “high-level” language to be invented, right after Fortran. John McCarthy considered using manual memory management or reference counting, but eventually settled on (and coined) garbage collection — once the program was out of memory, it would go back and find unused storage it could reclaim.

The gray stack is empty, and every object in the heap is either black or white. The black objects are reachable, and we want to hang on to them. Anything still white never got touched by the trace and is thus garbage. All that’s left is to reclaim them.

**About:** Section: 26 . 7 . 2 Interning strings. Here’s another similar one. All strings are interned in clox, so whenever we create a new string, we also add it to the intern table. You can see where this is going. Since the string is brand new, it isn’t reachable anywhere. And resizing the string pool can trigger a collection. Again, we go ahead and stash the string on the stack first.
